**2026-11-21, R4, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-r4-finding-claims-not-evidenced.** Four claims have no test: C30 (reinstatement by event), C44 (decision and event times injected), C48 (procedures retired on drift), C50 (Gate D's dogfood). C44 was first stated as "nothing in packages/core reads the clock", which is false: `packages/core/src/db/postgres-worker.ts` (lines 34, 41, 54) and `postgres.ts` (lines 211, 229) call `Date.now()` for transport timeouts. It is restated as injected decision and event times, which no test or lint rule checks. `docs/conventions/testing.md` carries a one-line correction. Each of the four has a backlog row naming what closes it: a reinstatement surface first (C30), a lint rule (C44), card D3 (C48), the dogfood's reports (C50). The thesis states these as design intent, not evidenced properties.[^10][^11][^12]

**2026-11-21, R4, finding.** E2.7's characterisation tests, merged as `packages/core/test/counterexamples/trust.test.ts`, were read against every crosswalk claim. A test that pins a gap is evidence against a claim, not for it. None contradicts a claim as the crosswalk states it, but four needed their limits widened. C02: under a rule that admits its source, a co-member less trusted than an item's owner may rewrite, retire or evict that item (GH-38). C12: a sourceless write keeps its claimed kind and actor (GH-39). C13: content is model content only as a harness labels it. C18: a first write may carry grants, and with no source its claimed kind stands (GH-39). No characterisation test is named as evidence. When card E2.11's opt-in fix lands, these limits shrink under the policies that enable it.[^13][^14]

## Twin

**2026-11-06, E9.7, finding.** The first offboarding fixture promoted one of Dana's person notes into role:lead, reviewed by Sam, and the promotion was denied. Widening an active semantic item needs a reviewer who can read it (ADR 0026), and in a twin no one but its owner can read a person scope, while an owner may not approve their own item. Under the twin profile a person note never reaches a role by promotion; a person restates it in the role instead. The fixture keeps a fifth person note and no promotion. If Twin needs a person-to-role path, a grant to the reviewer or a self-approval rule for one's own role is a policy decision for the lead.[^15][^16]

**2026-11-13, E9.7, failure.** The first cross-backend test written in the review asserted that no explanation row is left after offboarding, and failed on both backends with 2 rows. Sam's read returned the two role items as well as Dana's granted note, and their rows rightly stay; only rows of erased items are removed. The test now counts rows of the erased items only, and passes on SQLite and Postgres. Nothing in the code; the acceptance test's own count of zero holds only because its fixture never reads.[^17]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** On the files adapter, a memory file Claude Code wrote cannot be granted: the adapter holds it read-only (`ReadOnlyItemError`), so a grant change on it is one deny event under `engram/write/store-failure`. The twin dogfood (card E9.5) runs over the files adapter on each member's Claude Code memory folder, so most of a member's person scope is such files. An item written through Engram (`engram write`, or the pi extension's `memory_write`) in the same folder can be granted, its file carries the grant, and adoption leaves it standing; an imported file refuses, and both the item and the file are unchanged (`packages/adapters/test/plane/grants-files.test.ts`, two tests). In E9.5 members grant items written through Engram. Granting Claude Code's own files needs grants held by the plane alone (adoption ignoring them) or a copy of the memory into an Engram item: a follow-up card for the lead (ADR 0034).[^18][^19]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding C1 of PR #44 (critical): a grant change could undo an erasure committed through another handle on the same log. `decideAndCommit` read the item, awaited the store write, then appended the allow event without reading the item again under the log's lock; the write queue orders calls within one db handle only. The reviewer reproduced it with two handles on one SQLite file: an offboarding on the second erased item 1 while the first's store write was held, and the allow event brought the content back, through a rebuild, with the store record recreated and the grantee reading it. Two tests reproduce it on `sqliteBackend.pair()` (an erasure, and a second grant change, on the other handle); both returned allow before the fix and pass after it. Mutants removing the re-check or the store repair each fail both. Fixed by decision `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-commit-under-lock.md`.[^20][^21]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding I1 of PR #44: `Plane.write` bypassed the owner rule of grant changes. A role co-holder refused by `changeGrants` could rewrite the owner's item through `Plane.write` at version plus one with the grant, and an outsider then read it; the item still named its owner, so the grant read as the owner's consent. Plan decision 4 makes a grant change a governed write, so this had to hold before the twin dogfood. `evaluateWrite` now judges a rewrite whose grants differ from the projected item's by the grant change's rules. Three tests (a co-holder's write that adds, removes or alters a grant is denied) and a property over rewrites fail without the owner check. Decisions `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-import-may-change-grants.md` and `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-grant-change-on-write.md`.[^22][^23]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding M1 of PR #44: `Plane.write` and `changeGrants` disagreed on what a grant may be. The write path accepted an expired grant and a duplicate, which `changeGrants` refused, and `changeGrants` accepted a grant of another class than the item's, which could never admit a read. `grantDefect` in `packages/core/src/policy/evaluate.ts` is now the one check; the outcome is decision `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-m1-first-writes.md`. Open question in ADR 0034 for the lead.[^24][^25]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding M2 of PR #44: the inspector showed a grantee every grant on an item it reads, so it learned who else may read it. The lanes listed `item.grants` as the read returned them. Fixed in the inspector only, as the review asked (decision `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-grants-shown.md`); the read path is unchanged (finding of the same day). A backlog row for the read path.[^26][^27]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding M3 of PR #44: ADR 0034 said the lead-owned invariants file was untouched, which was false. The surface check had been changed to classify `changeGrants` in the same PR, after the ADR was written. The ADR's consequences now list every change this card makes to the file and its oracles. None.[^28]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding M6 of PR #44: a member of a scope whose level may not read it learned the item's owner from a grant change's refusal. The opaque refusal was decided by membership, not by `readableScope`. Fixed (decision `2026-11-19-e9-4-review-decision-refusal-by-read-access.md`); the property asserts that such a refusal names neither scope nor owner, and a mutant restoring the membership test fails it. None.[^29][^30]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Review finding M7 of PR #44: ADR 0034 said a grant change is never an agent's, without saying that this holds only for agents going through MCP or pi. Until authentication exists the HTTP actor is self-asserted (ADR 0027), so any local process can post to `/grants` claiming to be a user. ADR 0034, decision 8 now says so. None beyond the existing authentication follow-up.[^31]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** A read returns every grant on each item it returns, so a grantee reading through the API, the CLI or MCP learns who else may read the item. Grants are fields of the item and the read path returns items whole. Not changed by card E9.4, as the review asked; ADR 0034, decision 9 says the API still returns them all. A backlog row: withholding other grantees' grants at read is a read-path change, and touches what explanations name.[^32][^33]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** ADR 0034's decision 5 said an offboarding commits under the same lock, so a grant change never brings erased content back; the verification of PR #44 showed that false as written. The re-check under the lock covers the grant change being the slow side. Offboarding deletes the store records before it takes the lock, so a writer committing in that window is not covered (orderings R2 to R4, the finding of the same day). Decision 5 and `docs/architecture.md` now say what the re-check covers and that the cross-handle gap stays open until card E2.6. None beyond card E2.6.[^34][^35]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, failure.** Verification finding V-4 of PR #44: a role co-holder could switch a dormant grant on, or a live one off, by rewriting the item to another class with its grants unchanged. A first write may carry a grant of another class than its item's (the M1 outcome), which admits no read; the I1 rule looked only at whether the grants changed, so a class change to that grant's class passed as an ordinary rewrite, and the grantee then read the item. Fixed by decision `2026-11-19-e9-4-verification-decision-class-change.md`; three tests failed before and pass after, and mutants dropping the class rule or the check of kept grants fail them. ADR 0034's premise that an other-class grant admits no read is now stated with its condition: while the item keeps its class.[^36][^37]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** A first write may name another principal as its owner, so a member of a shared scope can create an item that names another person as its owner and carries grants, which then read as that person's consent. ADR 0006's amendments keep the owner of a new item as written, not compared with the actor's id; the owner rule of ADR 0034 applies to changes of an existing item only. Found while correcting the sentence the verification of PR #44 asked to make true or correct. Not reproduced as a test; it follows from the write gate's documented rule. In the twin's person scopes only the owner is a member, so it concerns role, team and org scopes. A backlog row; `docs/architecture.md` and ADR 0034 now state it.[^38][^39]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** Offboarding deletes the store records before it commits its erasure, and three writers on another handle that commit in that window leave erased content readable (verification of PR #44, findings V-1 to V-3). The write queue orders one handle only (ADR 0007, N2). Orderings, on two handles of one log, twin profile: R2, offboarding deletes the record and is held before committing while a grant change commits on the other handle, rewriting the record. R3, an owner's `Plane.write` rewrite adding a grant is held at its store write while the offboarding completes. R4, the handover is held at its store write while the owner removes a grant, then writes the version it read, undoing the removal. In each the grantee `person:sam` reads what should be gone or ungranted; in-place promotion has the same shape. Not fixed here. Card E2.6 fixes every store-then-log path; three backlog rows.[^40][^41]

**2026-11-19, E9.5, finding.** Over the files adapter, the re-adoption that follows a plane write raises an agent-written item's provenance from `model` to `import`, and the gate then accepts its owner's grant on it. Card E9.4 found that plane writes are re-adopted once as `import`; for model content that re-adoption also defeats ADR 0021's decision D4, which refuses a grant on model content by the item's current kind. Seen first as a test failure: a grant on the agent's item, a day after its write, was allowed. A probe on a scratch folder: written as model, grant refused (`engram/write/grant`), one adoption (provenance now `import`), the same grant allowed. A promotion would read it as `import` too. A backlog row: adoption should keep the kind of a record the plane wrote. Until then the procedure says never to grant an agent-written item, and the report counts it under `watch`.[^42][^43]

**2026-11-19, E9.5, failure.** Review finding E95-13 (minor): the procedure did not say what a zero cannot show. An event records its actor's id, not its source, so the owner's agent posting a grant change through the route reads as the owner, and a first write naming another owner reads as that owner's consent. "What to send the team" states both; a test plants the first and records that the report gives 0. Recording an actor's capacity is the backlog row "An event's actor capacity".[^44]

**2026-11-19, RV3, finding.** The repository review's finding RV-ADP-4: adoption carries the grants of a file the store dropped, so a grant can outlive the record that held it. Left to card RV4, not fixed here. The fix belongs in core's `packages/core/src/plane/adopt.ts`, which card E2.6 is rewriting now; card RV3 touches the adapters and the no-bypass check only, and editing `adopt.ts` in parallel would collide with E2.6. Nothing changed for it on branch rv3. A backlog row records it for card RV4, to be taken after E2.6 merges, beside the row "Adoption raises trust", which waits on the same rewrite. Until card RV4, the twin dogfood's violation report is the check that a grant reaches nobody it should not.[^45][^46]

**2026-11-19, RV3, finding.** With imported ids now distinct per folder, one log still cannot hold two files folders under the same adapter name: the second folder's adoption is refused, because core keys a ref by adapter name and file name and both folders hold `files`/`user_role.md`. Found when the RV-ADP-2 scenario through the plane failed with "the store lists item ... at a ref item ... already holds", not with the retirement the review described. The refusal retires and writes nothing (test "under one adapter name, the second folder's adoption is refused and retires nothing"); with two adapter names (`files-dana`, `files-erin`) both members adopt and read their own items. Every surface opens the files store as `files` (`packages/server/src/workspace.ts`). The dogfood is unaffected (one folder and one log per member). A backlog row records the fix: an adapter-name flag, or refs that name the folder.[^47][^48]

**2026-11-19, RV3, failure.** Review finding RV3-1 (blocking): after an adopted files folder is moved, every adoption, and so every `engram read`, fails, while ADR 0013's amendment said the moved files are adopted again and the old ones retired. A move gives each file a new imported id at the same ref `files/<name>.md`. Core's `refProblem` counts retired holders too, so adoption throws before it retires what the store no longer lists. The amendment was written from the adapter's side without running a move through the plane. A test pins the real behaviour: two adoptions of the moved folder each throw and leave one `engram/write/store-failure` deny event, the projection is unchanged, and moving the folder back adopts with nothing changed. ADR 0013, `docs/dogfood.md` (a recovery note) and a backlog row for the core fix (card RV4, after E2.6) state it. Core is not changed here: card E2.6 rewrites `write.ts` and `adopt.ts`.[^49][^50][^51]

**2026-11-19, RV3, failure.** Review finding RV3-4 (minor): a case-only rename of an item file (`<ID>.md`) blocks rewrites of that item until the file is renamed back, which nothing stated. The renamed file names another id than its key, so RV-ADP-1 refuses it, and a write refuses a name that differs from the item's only in case (review finding R4-5). A test pins it: after the rename the item lists nothing, the renamed file is a diagnostic, a rewrite throws `UnsafeKeyError`, and after renaming it back the rewrite succeeds. `docs/dogfood.md` now warns against renaming an item file in any case, and ADR 0013's amendment names it. Nothing else; the behaviour is kept, since guessing which of two names is the item would be worse.[^52][^53]

**2026-11-19, RV3, failure.** Review finding RV3-5 (minor): the RV-ADP-1 entry and the PR body read as if tying an id to its file name stopped forged Engram files, which it does not. A file written by hand under the name of its own new id, with Engram metadata and a grant, is consistent with its name, so the adapter reads it and adoption takes it with its grants (the reviewer's probe `forge.mts`, part 2). That is RV-ADP-4, deferred to card RV4. The RV-ADP-1 entry is superseded by one that says plainly it stops copies and mismatched names only; ADR 0013's amendment and the PR body say the same. Forgery stays on the backlog row for RV-ADP-4.[^54][^55]

**2026-11-20, E2.6, failure.** Finding V4-2: a crashed promotion or derived write into a team or role scope whose bytes land after the sweep stays in that scope's store until something adopts that scope or a later offboarding sweeps it. Only the person scope is adopted, through `engram read`; no surface adopts a team or role scope. Not fixed here: ADR 0010 states the gap and a backlog row tracks covering it; the bytes are never projected or read through the plane meanwhile. A periodic sweep or `engram retain` over the scopes the log names would close it.[^56][^57]

**2026-11-20, E2.6, failure.** Finding V4-6: a Claude Code memory file the plane cannot delete is adopted again, under the erased id and with the erased text, once one line is appended, and Claude Code appends to these files routinely. ADR 0010's rule adopts a left-in-store record again once its content changes in any way. The rule is unchanged; the case is an open question for the lead in ADR 0010. The lead decides whether such a record is adopted only under a fresh id, or only without the erased lines.[^58]

**2026-11-20, RV4, decision.** Four backlog rows leave the backlog, marked done on branch rv4: "Re-adoption of plane writes", "Adoption raises trust", "Adoption carries grants from a dropped file (RV-ADP-4)" and "Adoption after a folder move". The backlog's rule is that taking an item off it is a `decision` entry. Card RV4 fixes all four in core's adoption. Each row names what was done and the ADR amendment that records it; the evidence column keeps its earlier entries and adds this one. The twin dogfood's precondition "the adoption fix has merged" is removed from `docs/dogfood.md` and from card E9.5's status line; three preconditions remain, for the lead.[^59][^60]

**2026-11-20, RV5, finding.** Claude Code's native auto memory reads and writes the very folder the twin dogfood governs, and its transcripts keep MCP results in plain text (finding RV-SRF-5). The review found `docs/dogfood.md` silent on both. Claude Code's docs (https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory and /claude-directory): auto memory is on by default, loads the first 200 lines or 25 KB of `MEMORY.md` into every session, and is turned off by `"autoMemoryEnabled": false` in any settings file, the `/memory` toggle, or `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1`. Transcripts under `~/.claude/projects/<project>/` are plaintext with tool results, deleted after `cleanupPeriodDays` (default 30); `CLAUDE_CODE_SKIP_PROMPT_HISTORY` stops them. `docs/dogfood.md` now has members turn auto memory off in `.claude/settings.local.json`, add a CLAUDE.md line, and states the transcript limit. Whether Gate D's "zero recorded violations" stands beside the ungoverned context and transcripts is the lead's decision (backlog row "Claude Code's native memory path").[^61][^62]

**2026-11-20, RV7, finding.** Review finding RV7-R6 (design): `Plane.changeGrants` takes no expected version. The owner can therefore grant an item whose text a co-holder rewrote while it carried no grant, text the owner never saw. RV-CORE-1 makes a rewrite of a granted item the owner's only. An ungranted item stays open to co-holders, as ADR 0006 allows, and a later grant change judges only who asks. No code change, as the review asked. This is a backlog row, "A grant over text the owner never saw", and ADR 0034's amendment names it. An expected version on the call, refused when the item has moved on, would close it. For the lead.[^63][^64]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e1-4-finding-e14-f3-oracle-plane-disagree.** Finding E14-F3, restated: the plane launders agent content into grantable content. Review finding E1.4-4. Dana saving her agent's identical text again as a user-sourced write makes the item `user`; `changeGrants` to Sam is allowed and Sam reads the agent's words verbatim, around ADR 0021's decision D4. A rewrite in her own words, or a promotion's new version of either, does the same. By the record the oracle is right: the E9.5 decision that a raised grant is a violation, ADR 0021's RV4 amendment, `docs/dogfood.md`; ADR 0034's decision 6 does not authorise it. Any such rewrite also makes `watch.provenance_raised` non-zero, which `docs/dogfood.md` says should stay 0. Pinned in four cases of the E14-F3 test; the relaxation filters only the owner's own raise. A core card, in the backlog: refuse adding or widening a grant on an item any allowed version of which held untrusted content.[^65][^66][^67][^68][^69]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding.** Finding E14-F6: after the owner's crashed rewrite or grant change of a role item carrying grants, a co-holder's adoption of the role denies the record and retires the owner's committed item for good. The reworked model's first default run (seed -19116893, path 179:2:1:4:4:4:4, replay path JACACAa:VB): Lee grants Sam his role item, his rewrite of it is killed after its store write, and Dana adopts the role. Dana's adoption is refused under `engram/grant/owner` (only the owner rewrites an item carrying grants) and adoption retracts Lee's item; Lee's own adoption afterwards changes nothing. Sam loses the read; the store keeps bytes the log denied. Strict runs also reach it through a killed grant removal (path 43:3:3:3:9:8:12:12:12) and through an item evicted before the adoption (path 1794:4:2:3:4:8:1:3:5:5:9:10:10:10:10). Pinned in the test named E14-F6. For the lead: recovery of a shared scope depends on who adopts it.[^70][^71]

**2026-11-21, E1.5, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e1-5-finding-purge-after-swept-id.** For the lead, review finding E1.5-8: the swept-id purge race is real in the code, and a committed write is lost at the next adoption. The review reproduced `good::sweptIdPurgeRaceTest` on two SQLite handles of one log: adoption refuses a record under a swept id, a write under that id commits, the purge deletes its record, and the next adoption retires the item. Reachable wherever adoption runs beside a writer in another process: `before-read.ts` `syncBeforeRead` adopts on the owner's read (`engram read`, the pi extension before each prompt) while `engram serve` writes. A second path: the purge retry for bytes already refused (adopt.ts, the `decided` branch) deletes with no lock and no deferral. A core card should refuse new writes under swept ids, or check under the lock before each purge's delete; a backlog row names both.[^72][^73][^74][^75]

**2026-11-21, E1.5, finding.** For the lead, review finding E1.5-1: the sweep's put-back can leave erased content in the store after a third offboarding of the same person. When the sweep finds a record it deleted was rewritten, it puts the item back (offboard.ts, the loop after a changed swept record). A third offboarding can erase the item between the put-back's read and its write; the next read finds it gone and the loop runs `break` without deleting what it wrote. `knownGapThreeOffboardings::putBackAcrossThirdErasureTest` ends with every handle idle, both reports appended, and b's erased content in the store: `not(erasureHolds)`. The good model's bound of two offboardings hid it. A core card should delete the record when the item is gone or its scope is being erased, as settle's `wanted` does; a backlog row names it.[^76][^77][^78]

**2026-11-21, E1.6, finding.** For the lead: a verified chain vouches for the log only; an erasure that verifies may still have left content in a store (E1.5-1, the sweep's put-back), and the projection and explanation tables are not chained. The card's brief asks that the chain not hide the put-back leak. Every report carries the note that the chain vouches for the log only and points at the deletion report and the dogfood report. An edited items row is not detected by verify; rebuild() and the replay invariant are what judge the projection. A later card could compare the projection with a rebuild in engram log verify.[^79][^80]

**2026-11-21, E2.13, finding.** Item 36's rule (a non-owner sees only the grants naming them) is not built in core on main: `Plane.read` still returns every grant on an item, and only the inspector's `shownGrants` hides them. The card asked to check, and not to build it here if absent. Backlog rows "Grants seen by a grantee" and "Grants a non-owner sees" still say "decided, not yet built". `whoCanReadItem` applies the same rule to its own answer, so a grantee learns other holders from `Plane.read` today, not from this answer. Until item 36 is built, the leak property of this card bounds only what who-can-read reveals, not the read path. The card that builds item 36 can reuse `liveGrants` as this one does.[^81][^82][^83]

**2026-11-22, E2.13, finding.** Two leaks outside this card, confirmed by its review, recorded and not fixed. First, item 36 is a live leak on main: `Plane.read` returns every grant on an item to a grantee, so it learns every other holder; only the inspector's `shownGrants` hides them. Second, `evaluateGrantChange` (`policy/grants.ts`, the owner check near line 104) refuses a non-owner member of the scope with "only its owner <id> may change the grants", naming the owner even of an item closed to that member. Card E2.13 is scoped to who-can-read; both are read and grant-change paths the lead owns. `whoCanReadItem` withholds both from its own answer. The read path and the grant-change refusal still give them. For the lead: item 36's backlog row, and a matching withholding in the grant-change refusal.[^84][^85][^86]

**2026-11-25, ANS-2026-11-25, finding.** ADR 0034's one-year expiry cap and first-write rule (item 34) are accepted, not built. The acceptance asks for the code to be checked. `grantDefect` (`packages/core/src/policy/evaluate.ts`) has no upper bound on `expires`, and `evaluateWrite` skips it for every first write (`if (!changesGrants || kept) continue;`). Two tests in `write-grants.test.ts` pin this: "a rewrite adding an expired grant is refused; a first write carrying one still lands" and, at lines 135 to 153, "a grant naming another class than the item's is refused by a rewrite adding it and by a grant change". Card E2.21 builds it; part 1 is on branch `e2-14-grant-cap`. Part 2 (the cap and first-write rule) lands, under the delegation, after H1's MERGE, behind a policy option `grants.expiry_cap` no registered preset sets: the cap would refuse 1,633 of 2,490 GateMem fixture writes. Part 2 must change both tests and the loop comment on `twin-reads.json`.[^87][^88][^89][^90]

**2026-11-25, E9.9, finding.** An imported or held episodic candidate in a person scope still needs a second member, and even then becomes a semantic version, never an active episode. ADR 0026 promotes an episodic item only to semantic, and ADR 0049 leaves that alone (C46). The round trip's two confirming episodes stay candidates after import, as before. For the lead: whether an owner may accept an episodic candidate as an active episode in place, which is a further ADR 0026 change.[^91][^92]

**2026-11-25, E9.9, finding.** The TUI on branch `cliupgrade` labels `--review` "Approve as reviewer" in its Promote form, and its handoff of an import says a reviewer promotes. Since card E9.9 the same flag also lets an owner accept a candidate in their own person scope. The plain CLI's help now names both; the read and explain shapes are unchanged, and a promote event may carry `engram/promote/owner-person`. The TUI should relabel the flag (for example "Approve, or accept as owner") and show the new policy id; nothing breaks if it does not.[^93][^94]

**2026-11-27, RD1, finding.** Whether the grants shown of an item are all of them is still inferred outside core, for the label only (verification N2). Core's answers carry no such field and only card E2.8 changes the schema. `readWhole` infers it from an empty `grants_used`, which holds because core admits by scope before grants. The TUI's `knownAnswer` takes every answer that is not a read as whole, which is wrong for an owner who cannot read the item's scope and gets a promotion's answer. The grants listed are core's in every case; only the "every grant is shown" label can be wrong. New test `known-answer.test.ts` pins both branches of `knownAnswer` (4 passed); making the non-read branch not whole fails one. Backlog row "Grants shown, stated by core" asks core for the field, its own PR after H1. When the field lands, `shownGrants` takes it and `readWhole` goes.[^95][^96][^97][^98]

## Distill

**2026-11-21, D1, finding.** The bypass redactor's last rule, `\b[A-Za-z0-9_-]{32,}\b`, matches every canonical UUID, git SHA and long hash, and so does any long identifier made of letters, digits and hyphens. Card D1 runs the table over whole sessions, which are full of such strings. A bypass reason, by contrast, is one short command. In free text, such strings are replaced by `<redacted>`, for example a commit SHA in a `git log` result. The scrub exempts pi's own id shapes so that sessions are not held for their ids. Card E2.9, which unifies its guard with this table, should give the 32-character rule an entropy check or exclude hex and UUID shapes. Until then, episodes lose some evidence to over-redaction.[^99][^100]

**2026-11-22, D3, finding, H5.** `tau2Reward`, the replay's tau2 oracle, reads `db_match` alone, so a tau2 row with no database check (`db_check` null, `db_match` false) always fails. That is not H5's metric, `tau2_reward`, the product over the task's whole reward basis. Review finding D3-9, recorded for card X11. A task whose reward basis has no DB part scores 0 in the replay while tau2 may reward it 1; a task with DB and NL parts can pass the replay and fail H5's metric. Card X11 decides, with the lead, whether replay admission scores tau2 tasks by `tau2_reward` or by the database check, and states it before H5's runs.[^101][^102]

**2026-11-27, H5P, failure, H5.** The first draft counted 2 to 104 induction calls, where its own rule (at most one call per induction-half task) caps them at 52. 104 is the whole train list, or the old cost table's habit of counting induction under both C2 and C5, which the draft's shared library drops (review finding F1). The high bound moved from 1,029.69 to 1,027.45 billed dollars, the ledger high from 721.40 to 720.28, the reasoning sensitivity from 1,139.43 to 1,136.98; the low end did not move. Bounds reworked in the draft, the card and the cost entries.[^103][^104]

**2026-11-27, H5P, failure, H5.** The first draft asserted gpt-4.1's list-to-catalogue ratio as 1.0 and stated a ceiling of 901.75 ledger dollars, neither from a regenerable source. The ratio was read from pi-ai's catalogue in node_modules, while the cost table lists gpt-4.1 as unpriced and `test_run_limits.py` records no ratio for it; and the section's own Cost rule says ceilings are computed by the cost table, never in a proposal (review findings F2 and F3). The ratio is now a prerequisite (card H5P row 22), the ledger figures are conditional on it, and no ceiling is stated. The harness card records the ratio; the cost table computes the ceiling.[^105][^106][^107]

**2026-11-27, H5P, finding, H5.** The draft's low cost bound counts a judge call for every retail training and replay session, not only for tasks with natural-language assertions. Rechecking the arithmetic, the low total of 84.58 dollars matches only with 367 judge calls (148 retail training, 120 retail replay at 8 candidates, 99 evaluation). Counting the 99 evaluation calls alone gives 83.30 billed and 55.64 ledger dollars at the low end; the difference is about 1.29 dollars. The high end is unaffected. None for the draft, which now calls it an upper count: the low bound errs high by at most 1.29 dollars, and `COUNT=1` replaces it.[^108][^109]

**2026-11-27, H5P, finding, H5.** Card B11's read of tau2's code at the pin and the literature review disagree on whether retail's natural-language assertions count in its reward. Review finding F11: the draft relied on the code read without stating the conflict. PINS.md gives retail 112 of 114 tasks DB and NL_ASSERTION, every task run under `EvaluationType.ALL`, 40 listing assertions (11 of the 40 test tasks); the review's note says the assertions are off by default and not in the default `reward_basis`. The draft follows the code at the pin and now states the conflict. The harness card's fixture test checks the reward basis at the pin.[^110][^111][^112]

**2026-11-27, H5P, finding, H5.** The split counts and the two `split_tasks.json` hashes in the draft were read in card B11's tau2 checkout on 2026-11-25 and were not reread on 2026-11-27; only 12-character prefixes of the hashes are in the repository. No tau2 checkout is on this machine's `.engram/upstream/` today, and fetching one was outside this pass. Airline 30 train and 20 test of 50, retail 74 and 40 of 114, as the draft states; no script regenerates them yet. The draft now calls the hashes prefixes (review finding F11). The harness card's config records the full hashes and its test checks them at the pin.[^113][^114]

## Plane

**2026-10-22, S0.10, finding.** pi-ai 1.3.0's request options (`StreamOptions`, `SimpleStreamOptions`) carry temperature and maxTokens but no sampling seed. ADR 0016 and AGENTS.md rule 6 assume every sampled call takes a seed from the experiment config. The seed cannot be passed to providers through pi-ai. It is part of the cache key instead, so each seed is one recorded sample and reruns replay it exactly. Seeds give reproducible replays and independent replicates, not reproducible fresh samples; the methods chapter says so, and the optional seeded open-weights arm (roadmap X8) is the only bit-exact path.[^115][^116]

**2026-10-25, A1, finding.** Open-source Mem0 3.3.1 records history as memory text only (previous value, new value, event), not the metadata of each version, and its memory ids are generated by Mem0 on add. The Adapter contract's `history` returns full MemoryItem versions, and the plane's MemoryItem rides in Mem0 metadata. Mem0 history cannot rebuild a MemoryItem version, so the Mem0 adapter offers no `history` and claims `native_history: false`; the plane keeps versions. Because Mem0 picks the ids, the adapter finds an item's memory by an `engram_id` metadata filter before each write, and its store_ref key is the Mem0 memory id. ADR 0013 lists a Mem0 history endpoint; using it would need Mem0 to keep metadata per version.[^117][^118]

**2026-10-25, A1, failure.** The Mem0 adapter switched Mem0's telemetry off only when it first loaded `mem0ai/oss` itself, so a program that loaded Mem0 earlier kept telemetry on (review finding R4-7). Mem0 reads MEM0_TELEMETRY once, when its module is evaluated, and sends an init event, notice lookups and usage events to its analytics host. A child process that loaded `mem0ai/oss` before the adapter attempted five network calls and created its MEM0_DIR folder, in both import orders tried. Now a side-effect module imported first sets the variable, and each Mem0 instance has its telemetry and notice methods replaced by no-ops; both orders make zero calls and create no folder. Disabling the instance step alone makes the Mem0-first order fail again. AGENTS.md rule 12 holds for the Mem0 adapter whatever the import order; a Mem0 upgrade that renames these methods fails loudly.[^119][^120][^121][^122]

**2026-10-25, A4, failure, H4.** The conformance suite as first written for card A4 passed twelve adapters with planted bugs, among them dropping grants, nulling the validity window, truncating content to 200 characters, listing by scope prefix and accepting another adapter's ref on delete. Its corpus used only plain short text with empty grants and open validity, and most cases compared ids or a few fields, never the whole item. Run against the old cases, the meta-test failed on all twelve variants (12 of 13 tests failed); after the corpus and the whole-item comparisons were added, all twelve fail the suite and the honest fake passes it. The meta-test stays committed, so a weakened case or corpus fails the build. Store results on H4 rest on a suite that checks every field.[^123][^124][^125]

**2026-10-25, E2.1, failure.** The first syntax-error test for card E2.1, an unterminated double quote on line 11 of a policy, failed: the error was reported at line 31. The yaml 2.9.1 parser detects a missing closing quote only at the end of the input, and the loader reports the position the parser gives. The test now breaks line 16 with a nested mapping in a compact value, which the parser reports on its own line; the unterminated quote case is a known limit of the line numbers. Policy authors may see an end-of-file line for an unclosed quote; the message names the missing quote, which is enough to find it.[^126][^127]

**2026-10-25, E2.2, failure, H1.** With the real Plane in place, invariant 1 as written in step-0 task S0.9 passed while testing almost nothing: in its 300 seeded cases no read returned a single item. The same random policy gated the writes and the reads. Few writes landed (a level often has no rule; random source lists, classes and floors deny most items), two thirds of generated items are not active, and about half start after the read time, so every read came back empty. A probe of the seeded run counted 37 landed writes and 0 returned items. After the change in the decision entry of the same date, the run admits items by scope and by grant, and a guard fails the test if either count is zero. Any future property over the read path should count what it exercised, not only what it rejected.[^128][^129]

**2026-10-25, E2.2, failure, H1.** The first strengthened invariant 1 and the first read-leak property passed while still missing the cases they were written for. Colliding timestamps alone left too few grant boundaries: an expiry equal to the read time had to coincide with a grant naming the reader, of the item's class, on an active and valid item. Likewise hidden items rarely shared a searched scope and a query word with visible ones. With colliding instants only, the run met 0 grants expiring at the read time and the `>=` expiry mutant survived; the leak property met hidden search hits in 14 of 500 cases, and the relevance-over-all-hits mutant survived it. Generating cases around one chosen grant, and items sharing a common word in bridged scopes, killed both. A guard should count the exact condition a mutant needs, not a proxy for it.[^130][^131][^132]

**2026-10-25, PX1, finding, H1.** Pi's compaction and branch summaries send the session's messages to the model without the `context` event, so the memory tag never applies to them. Finding R7-15 suspected it from the bundle; pi 1.3.0's core/compaction/compaction.js serialises the messages to summarise with convertToLlm and calls the model directly. A session that read memory and then compacted sent the plain memory text in the summarisation request (the attack test failed on the PR's code). The extension now replaces memory results and recall messages with a notice in the arrays pi hands `session_before_compact` and `session_before_tree`, and the request carries none. That relies on pi summarising the arrays it passed the handler, which the test pins at pi 1.3.0. A pi upgrade reruns the compaction attack test before the pin moves.[^133][^134]

**2026-10-29, A2, failure.** The first `make test` after the letta doctor check landed failed one test, "doctor and write print no part of the secret", at the 30 s timeout; a rerun passed (91 files, 1903 tests). That test runs `engram doctor` once per URL case; the letta check adds about 650 ms per run on this machine (thirteen git processes), and alone the test went from 18.4 s to 21 s, which leaves little room under the load of the full parallel suite. Overlapping the letta check with the earlier checks saved nothing measurable (21.1 s) and was reverted. The test file is outside card A2's files, so its timeout was not raised. The test is load-sensitive on Windows; if it times out again, the fix is a per-test timeout or fewer doctor runs in it, in its own change.[^135][^136][^137]

**2026-10-29, A2, finding.** Git for Windows sets `core.autocrlf=true` in its system configuration, and each git process costs about 20 to 80 ms on this machine, so a MemFS write (add and commit) takes about 150 ms. Card A2 needed both facts to keep item bytes exact and the suite fast. LF-only item files are untouched by `core.autocrlf` on add, and a CRLF checkout is read back as LF (`packages/adapters/test/letta/letta.test.ts`). The Letta conformance cases take about 38 s here, the corpus cases about 2.6 s each; the registry copies one initialised template repository per case instead of running `git init`. Every later suite that writes many items through the Letta adapter shares the template (`freshLettaRepo`) and keeps its corpus small; Linux CI spawns faster.[^138][^139]

**2026-10-29, A2, finding.** Two git clients committing in one working tree can revert each other: a plain `git commit` reads HEAD, then the index file, and an Engram commit landing between the two (ref updated, index not yet renamed into place) is reverted by the other client's commit. Found while making the R2-1 race test deterministic: one run showed a user commit, parent Engram's commit, deleting the item Engram had just added. About one run in fifteen of sixteen writes against a tight commit loop (`race.test.ts`); git's ref compare-and-swap protects Engram's commits (seen as "cannot lock ref 'HEAD'" refusals), not the other client's. No adapter-side step closes it. ADR 0028 states it; a Letta agent and Engram writing one checkout at once is a limitation for the thesis.[^140][^141]

**2026-10-29, B4, finding, H1.** Under `medium` the plane admits every MEM-INV-Bench channel's entry and the poison reaches the model's context; the plane does not gate the action. H1's poisoning claim must rest on what the plane decides. Every channel's write is allowed (`plane.test.ts`), and the C5 cassette's last request carries the poison inside the read's memory tag (`mem-inv-probe.test.ts`). The HIT under C5 is the scripted reply and says nothing about the plane: the review set web's kind to `model`, the plane denied the poison, and C5 still scored HIT. Gaps: no untrusted-external kind; L-c passes because shared semantic `document` writes are admitted outright; L-b passes because ADR 0026's order ranks `tool` below `document`, so inheritance leaves the echo `tool`. Under `strict`, shared entries wait for review. The arm is stated as admission and audit; an untrusted kind is a candidate for the next schema PR.[^142][^143][^144][^145]

**2026-10-29, E0.4, failure.** The first Postgres wiring of E0.4 leaked secrets in three ways, and its redaction tests could not have caught them. - Redaction replaced only the WHATWG password. - An unparseable URL hung for 60 s, then crashed quoting it. - `?password=` values and `postgress://` paths were printed as given. - Removing the redaction from `openDb` left every test green. - Redaction is string-based and covers the userinfo and secret-like parameters. - Bad URLs are usage errors before any connection. - A test makes the server echo a secret (`password_encryption=...`). - A first attempt used a hand-built bad URL. It passed without the redaction, since core's error no longer quotes the URL, so it was replaced. A redaction test must exercise a path where a secret really reaches a message.[^146][^147]

**2026-10-29, E2.4, failure.** PR 4 passed every gate and its mutation checks, yet the independent review found five important defects: utility eviction of the write that just landed, expired items holding budget slots, retired content plantable by writing an item as retired, eviction reasons leaking to non-readers, and irreversible ttl retirement of shared scopes by any member's clock. On the way to fixing them, the manual-ask check first queried a `policy_id` column the events table does not have. The tests encoded what the ADR said, not what a full scope, a hostile member or a skewed clock does to it; the invariants' oracle mirrored the same reading. Each defect now has a test that failed on the reviewed code; the column query reads the event body instead. Adversarial probes (a hostile member, a wrong clock, a full scope) belong in the acceptance tests of every policy card.[^148][^149]

**2026-11-06, E8.9, failure.** The workload shape card E0.3 declared, the events of a run, could not carry the simulation card E8.9 asks for. `checkConsistency` puts an item only on allowed changes, a read event records counts and not its query, and events name an actor's id without its memberships, so neither a denied write nor a read can be decided again under another policy. Found while designing the replay, before any code: a stricter and a looser preset could only be compared on writes both allowed. The interface was replaced (ADR 0032), and the surfaces now record calls. The interface stubs of E0.3 for lint and distill should be checked against their cards before L1 and D2 build on them.[^150][^151]

**2026-11-06, L1, failure.** `make test` failed before any L1 change and still fails after it, in tests L1 does not touch. Two kinds of failure. Letta adapter and runner b3 tests time out (about 30 s on git-backed Letta operations, 300 s in b3) while another session runs tests on the same machine. And packages/core/test/unit/schema.test.ts and packages/runner/test/retrieval.test.ts cannot load: they build paths from a file URL, so the space in "Graduation Project" stays `%20` and the folder is not found. Baseline: 6 of 139 files failed, 8 of 2501 tests. After L1: 4 of 141 files failed, 2 of 2540 tests, the same two path errors plus timeouts in letta conformance and b3. Every L1 test passed. The path bug needs its own fix (decode the URL with `fileURLToPath`); it is reported, not silenced, and out of this card's scope.[^152][^153][^154]

**2026-11-06, PX2, failure.** In one full parallel `vitest run` on the Windows build machine, the Letta test "four openers race to initialise it; every one opens and every write lands" (review M-2) failed. Unknown; the test races four git initialisations of one folder, and the run had every package's tests in parallel. PX2 changes no adapter code. Run alone three times, the file passes 34 of 34 each time; the earlier full runs on branches `e8-9-simulator` and `schema-v1` passed it. The testing convention asks for quarantine with an issue link the same day; the lead opens the issue, since this machine has no GitHub CLI.[^155][^156]

**2026-11-06, PX2, failure.** The first replay design loaded the bundled preset a session's policy id named, and the E2.4 session failed to replay: three events differed. That session ran a one-slot variant of medium (a semantic budget of 1) under the id `preset.medium`; the bundled medium allows 2000, so the eviction and the retired-content denial after it were not made again. A policy id and version do not pin a policy's rules. The session now records the whole policy once (`engram.policy`), and the replay uses it. Anything that names a policy by id alone (event `policy_id`s, run labels) identifies a rule, not its numbers; comparisons across runs need the policy itself.[^157][^158]

**2026-11-06, PX3, failure.** `make test` failed on this machine before PX3 changed anything (39 tests in 17 files) and after (13 tests in 11 files), none in `packages/pi-package`. Three causes outside the card: two tests read fixture folders from an unescaped file URL, so the space in `D:\Graduation Project` becomes `%20` (`schema.test.ts`, `retrieval.test.ts`); `b3.test.ts` sees temporary Letta folders of other sessions running tests at the same time; and Letta lock and `EBUSY` contention plus 30 s timeouts under that load. Rerun alone, those files still fail in 4 places (the two `%20` suites, `b3.test.ts`, one Letta lock); the doctor tests pass. The PX3 tests pass in every run. `make test` needs a checkout path without spaces, or the URL fix in its own PR.[^159][^160][^161][^162]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** An item the plane writes into a files-adapter folder with source `user` is re-adopted once by the next adoption of that folder: one more allowed write event, its provenance kind becoming `import`. Adoption compares the projected item with the store's item as the adopter would record it (`withPlaneProvenance` with source `import`), so the projected `user` provenance never matches. It predates grant changes: a probe with no grant change showed the same (one re-adoption, then none). Found by the first run of `packages/adapters/test/plane/grants-files.test.ts`; the test now settles that re-adoption first, and the grant survives it, since the store's copy carries it. `engram read` and the inspector's lanes adopt before every read, so each Engram-written item gains one `import` write event after it lands. A follow-up for the lead: compare provenance without its kind, or skip items whose last write the plane made.[^163][^164]

**2026-11-19, E9.4, finding.** `Plane.promote` has review finding C1's pattern for in-place promotions: it awaits the store write and appends its allow event without reading the item again under the log's lock. The reviewer of PR #44 pointed at `packages/core/src/plane/promote.ts` and asked for it to be recorded, not fixed in card E9.4 (one card per PR). Not reproduced here; by the same reasoning, an erasure or change committed by another process during the promotion's store write is undone by its allow event. A backlog row (`docs/tasks/backlog.md`, follow-ups from card E9.4): the fix is ADR 0034, decision 5's, applied to promotion.[^165][^166]

**2026-11-19, L3, finding.** eventVisibleTo shows an event to any member of its scope, even a member the read matrix stops from reading that scope. Reported by the L3 review. Plane.lint refuses such a member, but the lint events another member caused are still shown to them; model reasons now restate in-scope content, which raises the stakes. eventVisibleTo in packages/core/src/visible.ts tests membership of scope_from or scope_to only, with no readableScope check. Backlogged in docs/tasks/backlog.md, not fixed in card L3: a change to visible.ts touches every surface that shows events.[^167][^168]

**2026-11-19, L3, finding.** Plane.lint keeps the log's write queue locked while the model answers every unit. Reported by the L3 review. The calls run in the lint's queue slot so the findings describe one projection and nothing is appended if a call fails; a server with a lint model would stall every write for the whole lint. No surface injects a lint model today: the runner lints scratch logs, the server and CLI none. Backlogged in docs/tasks/backlog.md: settle it (snapshot, release, then append with a staleness check, or a lint outside the queue) before a server injects a lint model.[^169][^170]

**2026-11-19, L3, finding, H3.** pi-ai 1.3.0's chat-completions adapter does not read the provider's `refusal` field, so a structured-output refusal reaches a ModelCall as empty text. The reply parser needs to tell a refusal from malformed output. In pi-ai's dist, `refusal` appears in anthropic-messages.js, openai-decisions.js, openai-responses-shared.js and compat-schema.js, and not once in openai-completions.js, the API the runner seeds; the parser therefore reads an empty reply as a refusal. A refusal written as prose ("I can't help with that") is counted malformed, not refused; both are unjudged, so only the split between them is affected.[^171]

**2026-11-19, RV3, finding.** The Letta adapter's `engram/MEMORY.md` keeps one line per item, retired items included, each with the item's title, the same exposure RV-ADP-3 removed from the files adapter, though only on demand. Seen while amending ADR 0028's comparison of the two file stores: `indexLine` puts the title in every line, and a retirement is a rewrite that keeps the line; Letta reads `engram/MEMORY.md` only when the agent opens it. Not changed: the card names RV-ADP-3 for the files adapter, the dogfood runs on the files adapter, and the Letta index is read on demand rather than loaded into every prompt. A backlog row: drop the title from the Letta index line, or the line of a retired item.[^172][^173]

**2026-11-19, RV3, failure.** Review finding RV3-2 (blocking): the escaped spelling of a loopback share in a posix shell command was not blocked, and in Git Bash it wrote into the store root, for every loopback host, `\\localhost` included, already on main. Git Bash, Claude Code's Bash tool on Windows, reads `\\` as one backslash, so the command text that reaches `\\localhost\C$\x` is `\\\\localhost\\C$\\x`. My tests used single-backslash spellings, which bash turns into `\localhost...`, so they never tested what the shell passes on. Run in Git Bash on a scratch store before the fix: six of eight spellings (`localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, `\\?\UNC\localhost`, `\\.\UNC\localhost`, `\\?\UNC\127.0.0.1`, `0--1.ipv6-literal.net`) wrote a file into the store and the hook allowed all eight; `127.1` and `[::1]` did not resolve on this machine. Fixed in the next entry. Shell tests now use the text a shell is given.[^174][^175][^176][^177]

**2026-11-20, E2.6, failure.** Finding V4-7: the offboarding's sweep makes one `adapter.list` call for every scope the log names. The sweep lists every scope to find records whose lineage traces to erased content. Not changed; a backlog row records it. An index of records by lineage, or a bound on the scopes listed, would cut it.[^178][^179]

**2026-11-20, RV4, finding.** The verification of RV4's second fix pass approved invariants 6 and 7 and left three minor findings. Two of them correct what the fix pass's decision entries claim. R-1: the kind floor does not record a person's restatement of retired agent content at the agent's kind, as the decision entry `2026-11-20-rv4-decision-review2-floor-from-retired.md` says. The retired-content rule refuses it. R-2: the owner's denied edit stays in the file only until the plane next writes that item, not indefinitely as `2026-11-20-rv4-decision-review2-match-over-projection.md` says. R-3: a dropped hit still takes a slot of a capped store search. The ADR 0004 amendment now states all three. R-1 and R-3 are backlog rows. None of the three affects the twin dogfood as specified.[^180][^181]

**2026-11-20, RV4, finding.** With adoption keeping only grants the plane's item carries, the stale writer's narrowing (`engram/adopt/narrowed`, card E2.6) has nothing to remove in the orderings it was built for. Narrowing removes grants an adoption of stale bytes carried that the version before it lacked; an adoption's grants are now a subset of that version's, so the set is empty. Orderings V9 and Q3b of `cross-handle-verify.test.ts` now show no narrowing event, the adoption's own reason naming the dropped grant. The code stays as defence in depth; the dogfood report still judges a narrowing if one appears. The lead may remove the narrowing in a later card, or keep it. Nothing changes here.[^182][^183]

**2026-11-20, RV7, finding.** The write rule's confidence floor compares `item.confidence`, a number the writer chooses, with the rule's floor. A model-sourced write passes any floor by claiming 1 (core review finding RV-CORE-8). The review asked that this be recorded for the lead, with the behaviour unchanged. `evaluateWrite` (`packages/core/src/policy/evaluate.ts`, the `below-floor` check) reads the item's own field. Nothing in the plane derives or caps it by source. The twin profile's floors (0.3 person, 0.6 role, 0.7 team, 0.8 org) therefore stop only a writer that reports low confidence honestly. Source isolation and the source lists, not the floor, are what keep model content out of shared scopes. A backlog row, "Confidence the model chooses". The thesis should not describe the floor as a guard against a model's overconfidence.[^184][^185]

**2026-11-20, RV7, finding.** RV-CORE-10 was reproduced in part: a files-adapter record at `schema_version: 0` is never migrated. The review guessed that adoption refuses each such record with a schema deny while the projection keeps serving it. What actually happens is worse for the person. The adapter skips the file as unreadable, with the schema error in its diagnostics. The next adoption then retracts the projected item: one allowed `retire` (`engram/adopt/retract`) whose reason quotes the schema error. The item leaves every read. The test pins this, so a fix must change it on purpose. No fix here: upgrading on read belongs in the adapter or in `adopt.ts` (card RV4's file). The backlog row "Version 0 workspaces" is widened.[^186][^187]

**2026-11-21, A5, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-a5-finding-honesty-cases-necessary-not-sufficient.** The `native_validity` honesty cases are necessary conditions only, and a meta-test now shows it: the fake, which keeps no validity of its own, passes all four with the flag forced on. The earlier entry rested on a throwaway probe and cited ADR 0031 and 0035; judging validity at the reader's time is ADR 0005 with its RV5 amendment (`valid_at`). The probe found fake, Mem0, files and Letta passing every case they ran with the flag forced on, because each stores the whole item. That holds for items carrying Engram metadata only: the files adapter derives `valid_from` from the modification time of a file without it, and would fail. The meta-test pins the fake's case. "Native" is not observable through the contract without an as-of argument; the ADR 0013 amendment says so, with both qualifications.[^188][^189][^190]

**2026-11-21, A5, finding, H3.** A replacement that states the supersession in prose only, with no lineage, status or window on the old item, leaves the old item admitted on every store. This is the paper's "indirect insertion", where the system must infer supersession from text; the plane reads supersession only from recorded fields and events. Pinned in the grid's "prose only" row: all nine revoked policies are admitted beside their replacements on all six stores. This is the unkeyed-item wall the literature note on 2609.08258 names, expected rather than new. H3 must keep keyed and unkeyed items apart; a model-based contradiction lint (card L3) is the only check that can see this case.[^191][^192]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding.** Finding E14-F2: a first write into a person scope, decided before an offboarding and killed before its settling deletes its bytes, lands in the offboarded scope at the next adoption as the person's import. Crash-window case D5: the writer pauses before its store write, another connection completes an offboarding, the writer writes, finds its decision stale and is killed at the settling's delete. A live writer here is refused and deletes its bytes (R3 of cross-handle.test.ts). Killed, it leaves bytes under an id the log never recorded, after the report, which adoption cannot tell from a new memory (V-5). This is the open question ADR 0010 and offboard.ts name, now reached by a deterministic crash. Pinned in the test named `D5 (finding E14-F2)`. For the lead before the tag: whether a completed offboarding refuses such records, for example by recording decided but unsettled writes.[^193][^194]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding.** Finding E14-F4: after crashes, recovery by adoption can need two passes, not one, when a pass's own budget check changes an item it listed. The deep run's second counterexample (4,000 runs, seed -19116893, path "3410:3:2:9:9:8", shrunk five times), under a role budget of one: a role write killed before its commit leaves item 4's bytes; item 5 is written with a grant; a grant removal on item 5 is killed after its store write. The first adoption records item 4, whose budget check evicts item 5, so item 5's listing is stale and deferred (P50-7). The second adopts item 5's narrowed record, which evicts item 4. The third appends nothing. Every invariant holds throughout. The main property allows three passes; the test named `E14-F4` pins this one. Minor. Surfaces adopting on every read converge; a one-shot recovery should loop until a pass appends nothing.[^195][^196]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e1-4-finding-e14-f1-store-keeps-grant.** Finding E14-F1, widened: a write or grant change that adds a grant and is killed or failing after its store write leaves the grant in the store's record for good; adoption drops it from the projection only. Review finding E1.4-5: a live store failure after persisting (`store.write:after` raising once) on a grant change does the same as a kill (crash case G4). E1.4-6: the recorded counterexample was out of date. Today `ENGRAM_STATEFUL_STRICT=F1` fails at seed -19116893, path 20:2:6:8:0:0:0:0, replay path BABAEAEABJIAZAD:VVF, shrunk to `[tight, write(dana, new person:dana #0, grant, crash store-after)]`. No reader is let in, and invariant 7 holds; store and projection disagree on grants after recovery. Pinned in tests named E14-F1 and crash cases W2b, G1, G4. For the lead: whether adoption, or the owner's next write, should rewrite such a record.[^197][^198][^199]

**2026-11-21, E1.4, finding.** Finding E14-F5: a stale writer killed before its settling writes the committed version back leaves its own refused bytes in the store, and the next adoption records them as the owner's import over the committed write. Review finding E1.4-3. A pauses before its store write; B's rewrite commits; A writes, finds its decision stale, and is killed at the settle's write-back (`settle` in plane/commit.ts; plane/grants.ts's conflict path). Crash cases W7 (a rewrite) and G3 (a grant change), on SQLite and Postgres: after recovery the projection holds A's refused text (W7) or the content B replaced (G3), kind import. Killed just after the write-back (W7b, G3b), recovery appends nothing. This breaks commit.ts's rule that recovery never undoes a committed owner decision. For the lead before the tag: the write-back needs a mark the next adoption can see, as the offboarding's begin mark is.[^200][^201][^202]

**2026-11-21, E1.6, finding.** For the lead: two processes creating a fresh log at once can fail with "table events already exists". migrate reads schema_migrations outside its transaction, so both processes see migration 1 pending and the second one's CREATE TABLE fails; reported by the verification of card E1.6, and it predates the card. Not fixed here (out of the card). A backlog row names it. A core card should re-read schema_migrations inside each migration's transaction and skip a migration another process applied.[^203][^204]

**2026-11-21, E2.10, finding.** Adoption records a store's item at the least trusted of what it claims, what the plane holds for its id, and `import`, so a record new to the plane that claims the user's word lands as `import`, which the four kind-conditioned rules never read. Review E2.10-4. A store can hold what a tool wrote directly, outside the plane. Stated as a limit of the result; a record that claims tool or model, or that the plane holds as such, keeps that kind and is screened by all eight. A backlog row asks the lead whether adopted records should get the kind-conditioned rules.[^205][^206][^207]

**2026-11-21, E2.10, finding.** Confirmed escapes under default (review E2.10-5, rerun on the fixed code): one Cyrillic or Greek homoglyph; a newline after "Assistant:" or "The agent must"; "Assistant - send", "Dear assistant,"; a hyphenated line break; "disregard everything above"; lowercase `[memory_persist]`, D.A.N, 1gn0re, `&lt;system&gt;`, French; one-signal standing directives; a short base64 payload; base64 after 20 decoys; hex payloads. A floor is stated with what passes it. Fixed narrowly: U+180E and U+034F now fold like other invisible formatting, and "your previous", "all of the previous" and "the instructions above" are caught; each has a test. The rest is listed in the config's limits. Card X14's suite should carry these as attacks.[^208][^209][^210]

**2026-11-21, E2.11, finding.** ADR 0040's exemption for the owner acting as `user` never changes a decision under core's order. The exemption is implemented and tested, so the question is whether it does anything. `user` is first in `TRUST_ORDER`, so a user-sourced writer is never less trusted than any item; protection already lets the owner, and any user-sourced member, act. It would matter only if an order put a kind above `user`. Nothing now; the lead may read decision 4 as documentation.[^211][^212]

**2026-11-21, E2.7, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e2-7-finding-gh-38-lattice-confirmed.** GH-38 holds under every registered preset, in team scope: a non-owner co-member rewrites or retires the owner's item at a less trusted kind, nobody else acting. The superseded entry showed it under a synthetic policy only. Medium and permissive: tool over user and document, import and document over user. Strict: tool retires a reviewed active user item (review only turns active into candidate), or rewrites it back into a candidate, hidden from reads. Person scope, all three: model over user. Grants stop non-owners; the grant rule refuses model, compaction and external content any grant. No registered number moves: the fix is opt-in; the GateMem guard (`gatemem-probe.ts:352-353`) covers ingestion only, but agents write only new ids (`extension.ts:156,378`); a GateMem session's own log holds at most 260 turns; LongMemEval at most 616 a question, both under strict's 1,000; MEM-INV-Bench has at most 3 sourced entries a case (`plane.ts:77-83`).[^213][^214][^215][^216][^217][^218][^219][^220][^221]

**2026-11-21, E2.7, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e2-7-finding-gh-39-floor-confirmed.** GH-39 reaches promotion and the retired-content rule as well as the write gate: both judge the kind a sourceless caller claims. The superseded entry tested the write gate only. Under medium, two sourceless writers claiming `user` confirm the owner's episodic item: the promotion is allowed (`preset.medium/promotion/episodic-to-semantic`, "3 confirmations by distinct actors"), its item `user`; labelled `model`, denied (`engram/promote/no-evidence`), since `confirmationsOf` (`promote.ts:67`) reads the claimed kind. A sourceless write claiming `user` restates evicted content, allowed; labelled `model`, `engram/write/retired-content`. Adoption and grant changes still hold the floor. Surfaces: `2026-11-21-e2-7-finding-gh-39-surfaces.md`. No registered number moves: every benchmark writer sets a source, no H1 to H4 config promotes, and C5's agent is `model`. C6 and C7 are not built (`config.ts:15-16`); their cards must set sources, or GH-39 reaches a registered condition (backlog row).[^222][^223][^224][^225][^226][^227][^228]

**2026-11-21, E2.7, finding.** How each surface sets the actor's source, the list of the GH-39 floor finding completed (it moved here for the word limit). Only a caller that sends no source reaches GH-39. CLI: `user` for write, promote, retain, lint (`setup.ts:86`; `commands.ts:338,410,501,546`); none for read and explain. A CLI or HTTP read is not write-free: the owner's sweep and adoption run first as the store's owner with `import` (`before-read.ts:87-91`; server `storeOwner`, `workspace.ts:486-488`). `engram doctor` writes as `user` (`doctor.ts:158-162`). MCP server and hook: `model` (`server.ts:115`, `hook.ts:130`). Pi: `model` for the agent (`extension.ts:224`), the session's actor as given for sweeps. HTTP: the body's actor, any source or none (`routes.ts:93`, `contract.ts:26`). The inspector reads with no source and changes grants as `user` (`data.ts:56,105`). Simulator: the workload's (`simulate.ts:242`). Adoption: `import` (`adopt.ts:199`). HTTP clients and library callers are the open paths.[^229][^230][^231][^232][^233][^234][^235][^236][^237][^238][^239][^240][^241]

**2026-11-21, E2.9, finding.** The bypass redactor (ADR 0014, R7-9) keeps vendor keys shorter than 32 characters in a bypass event's reason when no secret name or flag stands before them. Its last rule takes any run of 32 or more key characters; an AWS key id is 20, a Slack or GitLab token can be shorter, and `mem0 add AKIA...` keeps the id. Found while moving its patterns into the shared table; behaviour left unchanged by decision (bypass unchanged). Applying the guard's BLOCK-tier vendor rules in `bypassAttempt` would close it. For the lead: a small follow-up card, after the H1 live runs, since a bypass reason can reach a prompt.[^242][^243]

**2026-11-21, E2.9, finding.** Growth admission refuses gitleaks' `curl-auth-header` and `curl-auth-user`, admitted before; 210 of 222 rules are now admitted. Their `(?:.*?|.*?(?:[\r\n]{1,2}.*?){1,5})` before a header grows about 20 times from n to 4n in a backtracking engine (quadratic on a line of repeated `curl`). An Authorization header in a curl command is still found by the hand bearer rule; a `-u user:password` argument is not. Rewriting the two patterns by hand would admit them; a later card's choice.[^244]

**2026-11-21, E2.9, finding.** The invariants' oracle (`packages/core/src/oracle.ts`) does not model the content guards, and the invariant generators never set `content_guards`. The card's files leave the oracle alone, and the registered presets have no block, so the seven invariants exercise exactly what they did. `make test-invariants`: 8 of 8 tests pass. A policy with the guards on is covered by the unit and Plane tests of `secrets.test.ts`, not by the property tests. A card that registers an arm with the guards on (R2, X14) should teach the oracle the stage first, or the invariants say nothing about it.[^245][^246]

**2026-11-21, E2.9, finding.** Five gitleaks rules are left out for the backtracking bound: cisco-meraki-api-key, cohere-api-token, okta-access-token, privateai-api-token and sumologic-access-id. Each starts with a doubled lazy prefix, `[\w.-]{0,50}?(?i:[\w.-]{0,50}?(?:keyword)...`, harmless in RE2 and about 50 times slower in a backtracking engine. Linear, not exponential: 2,048 to 8,192 characters took 100 to 225 ms and 417 to 839 ms. The secrets they name go unrecognised except by the generic and heuristic rules. Rewriting the doubled prefix to one would admit them; that is a hand edit of generated data, so it is left for a later card.[^247]

**2026-11-21, E2.9, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e2-9-finding-upstream-divergences.** Ten vectors of the three sources come out otherwise here, on purpose, each pinned in `DIVERGENT` with its reason. The superseded finding counted six where the file held seven, and falsely said the walrus bearer value and the AWS example secret had been changed; the port now uses every source's own text, and three missing divergences are pinned (V-4). Not found: a Slack webhook with a 16-character path, `POSTGRES_PASSWORD=hunter2`, graphCTX's and walrus' cookie headers, `password=hunter2`, AWS's example key id, twenty of one letter, a short fine-grained token, `token = "..."` mid-sentence. Held, which walrus leaves alone: a build id with single capitals. A cookie rule and a shorter password floor remain choices for X14.[^248]

**2026-11-21, E8.11, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-e8-11-finding-lineage-in-payload.** Ancestor ids reach a viewer by two routes, not one: an item's `lineage` field, and the reasons core writes into events. Review finding E8.11-5: the earlier entry named only `lineage`. Reasons naming a lineage ancestor: write.ts:335 and write.ts:539, promote.ts:262, adopt.ts:568, offboard.ts:648, and the orphan lint's evidence at lint/det.ts:190. visibleEvents filters neither field. The inspector prints reasons in the event table's rows and tooltips, in the version diff's "made by event" line and in the event detail. The graph never makes a node from them. The provenance fixture has no such reason, so its leak test does not exercise them. No core code changed. For the lead's future core card: cut lineage to readable ids at the read, and reword these reasons for readers who cannot see the ancestor.[^249][^250][^251][^252][^253][^254]

**2026-11-21, E8.12, finding.** Three things later cards need to know. Each is outside this card's files or scope. First, the inspector sends no credential. On a server with credentials its page loads and every API call answers 401, so it works under `--insecure-loopback` only. Second, `engram mcp --http --server <url>` forwards with the MCP process's own `ENGRAM_TOKEN`, so that server refuses a session of another actor (`forbidden_actor`). Third, the tests that relied on tokenless loopback now pass `--insecure-loopback`: three in `packages/cli/test/client.test.ts`, and the `listen` and `serveHttp` calls in the server, mcp and e2e tests. The help snapshot and doctor's check list gained the new lines. A UI card gives the inspector a credential. Offboarding treats a token subject as it treats actor ids.[^255][^256][^257]

**2026-11-21, E8.13, failure.** Review finding E8.13-1 (critical): the reason filter split free text at `; ` and kept any fragment that matched a template, so `engram hook claude-code --export-jsonl` on the Bash command `mem0 add note; it targets person:zebra-quartz-7731` exported `[withheld]; it targets person:zebra-quartz-7731`. The slots of SCOPE, PRINCIPAL, POLICY and N admit most short tokens, and the filter judged every clause on its own, including those after free text. The content-free test planted the secret only in clauses no template could match. The review's reproduction gave `grep -c zebra` 1. It also leaked through `Plane.recordBypass` (`policy <secret>+x has no write rule for org`, `returned 41111111 of 2 candidates`) and a model-lint reason. No registered run is affected: no registered config names a sink. Fixed in the review pass (the change entry of that pass).[^258][^259]

**2026-11-21, E8.14, finding.** The middleware's recall queries with the raw last user text, so a last message full of markup recalls less: the fake store found nothing for a query mixing a forged tag with real words. Found while writing the forged-tag test, whose query carried the forged tag's words. The test now puts the forged text in earlier messages and asks a plain last message; defusing still applies to every message. Recall keyed on the last user message only is a limit the package README states. A later card could derive the recall query from more of the conversation; nothing is registered for it.[^260][^261]

**2026-11-21, R3, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-r3-finding-narrowed-replayable.** "An event log that replays a decision", the card's wording, is narrowed: Engram's log replays state, and a recorded workload replays decisions; the events alone do not re-decide. ADR 0032's context says so: a denied write's event carries no item, a read's event carries no query, and an event names the actor's id but not the level, memberships or source the gate decides on. `itemsAt` rebuilds the item set as it stood after any event from the log alone, except items later erased by offboarding, which never come back (`packages/core/src/replay.ts`; ADR 0010; `packages/core/test/replay/erasure.test.ts`). The simulator replays a recorded workload through the plane's own gate under any policy (`packages/core/src/plane/simulate.ts`). Replayable reads from the log alone are plan R-072. Supersedes the first version, which left out the erasure exception (review finding R3-7).[^262][^263][^264][^265][^266]

**2026-11-21, R3, finding, H4.** "Store independence proven by request identity", one of the four differences the card lists, is stated as a design property, not as a proven result. ADR 0035 decides that a request-identity check, comparing the sha256 of every model request across adapters, proves it, and that a card builds the check. No card has built it: `research/results/MANIFEST.md` has no row for it and no script produces it. The registered H1 runs use one store, Mem0 (`docs/research/preregistration.md`, H1 stopping rule). What holds now: one adapter contract and conformance suite over files, Mem0 and Letta (ADR 0013), and the design argument that the plane ranks in core and alone decides what reaches the model (ADR 0031, decision 2). When the request-identity check has a MANIFEST row, the positioning may say "proven" and cite it.[^267][^268][^269]

**2026-11-21, X15, failure.** Card X15's first measurement timed the three presets one after another on one log and store, so each preset ran on what the earlier ones had added (review finding X15-1). The suite populated once per store and size and reused it. At 100 items strict ran on 100 to 311 items, medium on 311 to 522 and permissive on 522 to 733, with 0, 211 and 422 earlier reads in the log; reversed, strict was the slowest. The preset comparison, the band's rationale (a spread put down to machine load) and the architecture paragraph read run order as noise. The probe also counted one statement fewer on a strict read, because strict ran first, on a handle that had not yet read the log's head. Every cell now runs on its own copy of the populated store; the suite was rerun and the result recommitted.[^270][^271][^272]

**2026-11-21, X15, failure, supersedes 2026-11-21-x15-milestone-rerun-check.** After the review fix pass was committed, the MANIFEST row of `research/results/h2-perf-budget.json` reproduced (`reproduce.check_row`: no problems), but the final `make perf-budget CHECK=1` failed. The aggregate band is two-sided and was set from one noise run. This rerun ran faster than the committed one (11 minutes, not 16; about 34 node processes on average, not 41). Every per-cell median stayed inside its band (the largest move was 2.37 times, the fake's permissive read at 1,000 items) and every query count and starting log size was equal. The read's geometric mean of median ratios was 0.644, outside 1/1.5 to 1.5; writes 0.790, denied writes 0.841. The noise run had given at most 1.13. The bands were not widened after seeing this. The lead or the next pass chooses among: a one-sided aggregate (a regression guard), ratios to the same run's bare baseline, or bands from several noise runs.[^273][^274][^275][^276]

**2026-11-21, X15, finding.** The first read time check on a new Db handle reads every event in the log. Card X15's review pass looked for why the probe counted fewer statements on a strict read than on the others. `logHead` in `packages/core/src/plane/read.ts` keeps a high-water mark per Db handle; a handle without one runs `SELECT seq, id, body FROM events WHERE seq > -1` and parses every event's body. A process that opens the log afresh, as each in-process `engram` command does, pays one scan of the whole log at its first plane call; later calls on that handle read only new events. The suite now takes that scan on the probe's handle before counting, and the warm-up takes it on the timed handle. A backlog row; the CLI's latency grows with the log even for one call.[^277][^278]

**2026-11-21, X15, finding, supersedes 2026-11-21-x15-finding-files-search-linear.** The files adapter's search reads every file in the folder on each read; the first entry's large spread across presets was the growing folder, not machine load. Review finding X15-1: in the first run each preset's cells saw the files earlier timed writes had added, up to 733 at "100 items".
